For the souls that have thus experienced Shiva Punya, the inclination towards Shiva Punya arises, and it becomes a cause for performing more Shiva Punya. Shiva Punya is of four types: Chariya, Kriya, Yoga, and Gnana. Among these, Upaya and Unmai (truth) are each of two types. These are further divided into Bhuddhi Purvam (intentional) and Abuddhi Purvam (unintentional), making a total of 16 types. Abuddhi Purva Upaya Chariya is when wealth and other things acquired as Pasu Punya (merit for living beings) become helpful to Kriya (ritual), Guru (teacher), Linga (symbol of Shiva), and Jangama (moving devotees). Abuddhi Purva Upaya Kriya is like the blind woman who performed Vilva Archana on an old Shiva Lingam at Gokarnam in the middle of the night. Abuddhi Purva Upaya Yoga is when a person who intends to steal sacred ornaments or a temple guard meditates on the sacred image itself. Abuddhi Purva Upaya Gnana is the desire for fame that arises from reciting Vedas and Agamas and clearly explaining their meanings to others. Now, Bhuddhi Purva Upaya Chariya is giving one's wealth to Kriya, Guru, Linga, and Jangama, and performing acts like sweeping and cleaning in the temple. Bhuddhi Purva Upaya Kriya is personally touching and worshipping those three forms. Bhuddhi Purva Upaya Yoga is believing that those three forms are the ultimate reality and meditating on them. Bhuddhi Purva Upaya Gnana is investigating the Vedas and Agamas, understanding that the Kriyacharyar (ritualistic teacher) is an invoked form, and realizing that Paramasivan manifests as Brahma, Vishnu, and other nine forms, and as the eight forms of the sun, moon, etc., and establishing oneself in that understanding with love arising for that truth. Abuddhi Purva Unmai Chariya is worshipping the Gnana Acharyar (spiritual teacher) without considering him as the supreme Shiva in human form, but only as a highly respected person. Abuddhi Purva Unmai Kriya is worshipping the Gnana Acharyar with the above understanding. Abuddhi Purva Unmai Yoga is meditating with the above understanding. Abuddhi Purva Unmai Gnana is investigating Gnana Sastras with the above understanding. Now, Bhuddhi Purva Unmai Chariya is worshipping the Gnana Acharyar with the clear understanding that he is the supreme Shiva. This is called "Kettal Gnanam" (knowledge gained by listening). Now, Bhuddhi Purva Unmai Kriya is worshipping the Gnana Acharyar with the above understanding. This is "Sindhithal" (contemplation). Bhuddhi Purva Unmai Yoga is meditating with the belief that he is the ultimate reality, by the grace of the Gnana Acharyar. This is "Thelithal" (clarification). Bhuddhi Purva Unmai Gnana is attaining the sacred feet by entering into Nishtha (meditative absorption). This Nishtha, which is Bhuddhi Purva Unmai Gnana, will have ten stages: Tattva Rupam, Tattva Darisanam, Tattva Suddhi, Anma Rupam, Anma Darisanam, Anma Suddhi, Siva Rupam, Siva Darisanam, Siva Yogam, Siva Bhogam. Siddhanta explains these as "Dasakariya Vilakkam" (explanation of ten stages). Among the 16 types of Shiva Punya mentioned above, by practicing the first 12, even though the two types of karmas, "hitam" and "ahitam", result in different outcomes of pleasure and suffering, both pleasure and suffering, merit and demerit, are alike in bringing about the result of birth and death. This leads to an equal outlook on merit and demerit. This is called "Suka Dukha Abhedam" (non-difference between pleasure and suffering), which is "Iruvinai Oppu" (equanimity towards the two karmas). When this arises, the intellect becomes firm in true Chariya and other practices. When this is attained, the influence of the "yan enathu" (I and mine) malam decreases, and an attachment to the supreme Shiva and a sense of servitude to the supreme Shiva arise. These are Malaparipakam (maturity of impurities), Shakti Nipatham (descent of divine power), and Sivagnanam (divine knowledge). When this arises, the soul attains the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. For such realized souls who have attained the sacred feet of Lord Shiva, since the sense of "I and mine" is absent, Agamya karma does not accumulate. For such Jivanmuktas, Agamya karma does not accumulate. Due to the manifestation of knowledge, the Sanchita karma, which is supported by the intellect, perishes like darkness before a lamp. Prarabdha karma remains as long as the body exists and is exhausted at the level of the body. Thus, when experiencing, the two karmas that arise as a cause of that experience do not lead to further Agamya karma. This is because the sense of "I and mine" is absent, and actions are performed with the thought that they are service to God. The Thirukkural emphasizes this: "Irul ser iruvinaiyum sera Iraivan porul ser pugazh purindhar mattu" (The two karmas that cause darkness do not affect those who praise God with true understanding). The experience that comes as Prarabdha also does not affect the Jivanmuktas who are immersed in the supreme bliss of Shiva. It comes, but it does not affect them. Regarding this, Sivagnana Bodham states: "avan arul allathu ondraiyum seiyanagave agnana kanmam pravesiya agalan" (Since he does nothing without His grace, ignorant karma does not enter). Thirukkural states: "Uzhaiyum uppakkam kanbar ulaivinri thazhadhu ugnatrupavar" (Those who strive tirelessly will see even fate turn its back). Therefore, from what has been said so far, the awakening that arises from the combination of Kaladi Tattvas is Karma malam, and the Agamya karma, which is the two types of karma, arises from performing "hitam" and "ahitam" due to that awakening. When that karma matures, it becomes Sanchita, which is merit and demerit, and when it comes to be experienced, it becomes Prarabdha, which is pleasure and suffering. When the Prarabdha, which is pleasure and suffering, is experienced, it will come to be experienced through the path of the two karmas. If one thinks that "I did these actions" or "others did them," then further Agamya karma will accumulate. Thus, these two types of karmas that occur lead to bondage. And there are 16 types of Shiva Punya for liberation from bondage. Among them, Bhuddhi Purva Unmai Gnana will have 10 stages. Those who practice these will attain the sacred feet of Lord Shiva and experience bliss. For them, Agamya karma will not accumulate, Sanchita karma will perish, and Prarabdha karma will be exhausted by experience. And that experience will not trouble those Jivanmuktas. Including all these truths, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman composes this Thiruppadal: "Seyvinaiyum seyvanum adhan payanum koduppanum meyvagaiyal nanku agum vidhitha porul enakkonde ivviyalbu Saivaneri allavatrukku illai ena uyvagaiyal porul Sivan endru arulale unarnthu arindhar" (He realized by grace that action, the doer, its fruit, and the bestower are truly four ordained entities, and that this characteristic is not found in paths other than the Saiva path, and that Shiva is the ultimate reality for salvation). Tolkappiyam and Sivagnana Siddhiyar state that there are eight elements for an action to occur. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman shows us the truth of Anava malam and Maya through this one concept of karma, by stating that the fruit of karma comes to the soul because God gave it to remove the darkness of the soul's Anava malam, and that God gives the soul the Tana (body), Karana (instruments), Bhuvana (worlds), and Bhoga (experiences) from Maya for the soul to experience these two karmas. And there is a doer of this action, which is the soul, or Pasu. We see that souls experience different pleasures and sufferings in different bodies according to different karmas. Therefore, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman explains the truth that there are many souls in the single term "seyvan" (doer). He refers to these as coming due to the fruit of karma in the term "vinaippayan" (fruit of karma). The types of karmas that come due to the fruit of karma, and the way they come, and the existence of many births, are all stated in this. Now, the soul has no independence, and therefore it is bound and associated with malam. It does not take its own fruits of karma; there must be one who bestows them. This is explained in the fourth term, "koduppan" (bestower). The divine Sekkizhar Peruman explains the Siddhanta conclusion that this bestower, God, will rescue souls from this cycle of karma, enlighten them, and then bestow blissful enlightenment, by explaining these four elements. In addition, he also refutes other religious views. This characteristic, these four elements, are exclusive to Saivism. Thus, the Nayanar realizes that Lord Shiva is the true reality and the path of Shiva is the true path, through the grace of God, due to the refutation of external doctrines and the conviction of Saivism, as stated so far. Therefore, the divine poetic skill of Sekkizhar Peruman, who, through this poem, has made us understand the entire truth of Siva Siddhanta, which includes the three entities of Pati, Pasu, and Pasam, and their characteristics, means, and benefits, is something we should experience and enjoy. Let us continue to ponder. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 04 — Sakkiya Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 918, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Sakkiya Nayanar Puranam. The sixth Thiruppadal, following the fifth Thiruppadal, is also a very, very important Thiruppadal. "En nilaiyil nindralum ekkolam kondalum manniya seer Sankaran thal maravamaip porul endre thunniya vedam thannaith thuravathe thuya Sivam thannai mikum anbinnal maravamai thalai nirpar." This Thiruppadal states that no matter what state one is in or what guise one adopts, the true essence is never to forget the sacred feet of Sankaran, who possesses enduring glory. Our Sakkiya Nayanar, having decided this, without abandoning the Sakkiya guise, the Buddhist guise, that he had adopted, excelled in never forgetting the pure emblem of Shiva with immense love. "En nilaiyil nindralum" (no matter what state one is in) appears here. This "nilai" (state) refers to one's condition. It can be interpreted as "samayam" (religion) or "asramam" (stage of life). Therefore, no matter what religion one belongs to or what stage of life one is in, and "ekkolam kondalum" (what guise one adopts), meaning what form or appearance one takes, "manniya seer Sankaran thal maravamaip porul" (never forgetting the sacred feet of Sankaran, who possesses enduring glory) is the true and ultimate reality for the soul, as the Nayanar understood. "Sankaran" means "one who bestows happiness." "Sam" refers to happiness and bliss. "Karan" means "doer." Thirunavukkarasar Peruman praises Sankaran's feet as "Arutchevadi" (feet of grace) in his Thevaram. He praises them as "azhagu ezhuthaga Arutchevadi" (beautifully written feet of grace) in Thiruvadi Thandakam. Thirunavukkarasar Peruman composed a Thandakam called Thiruvadi Thiruthandakam, praising the Lord's feet. Thayumanavar Swamigal says that whether it is the Lord's feet or His crown, they are both divine grace. Therefore, the sacred feet of Lord Shiva are an honorific term; they refer to the Lord's divine grace. "Maravathu iruthal" (never forgetting) means meditating continuously. In the Puranam of Vayilar Nayanar, we see "maravamaiyal amaitha manakkoyil ul iruthi" (establishing in the mind-temple built by not forgetting). This "maravathu iruthal" (never forgetting) is shown to us as the state of all Nayanmars in their Puranams. We see that never forgetting the Lord's feet is the state of true liberated souls. Ammai also prays, "maravamai vendum" (I need not forgetting). The 11th Sutra, second Adhikaranam, of Sivagnana Bodham states: "Ini bhaktiyinal maravathu etha avanathu seepathathai anaiyum endrathu" (Now, by worshipping without forgetting with devotion, one will attain His sacred feet). Sri Madhava Sivagnana Munivar explains this in the Mapashyam. Ichcha (will), Gnana (knowledge), and Kriya (action) naturally occur simultaneously. Whatever is known (Gnana) will simultaneously evoke Ichcha and action. Therefore, no matter in what order these three are stated, it is appropriate. However, in this 11th Sutra, since it states "ayara anbil aran kazhal sellum" (will reach the Lord's feet with tireless love), Sri Meykandar Devanayanar also states here "maravathu bhaktiyinal etha" (to worship without forgetting with devotion). "Ettuthal" means to follow the path of grace. Love, devotion, and passion, though slightly different in meaning, all refer to the arising of desire for something. Therefore, it is understood that devotion exists even in the state of supreme liberation. This is because, even in the state of supreme liberation, if the soul does not experience desire, then the experience of supreme bliss will not occur. Other traditions that do not understand this

Some traditions say that devotion is a means that the soul undertakes to attain the state of Jivanmukti. Siddhantins, however, argue that devotion is not only a means but is also required in liberation. Therefore, Sivagnana Bodham states that by worshipping without forgetting with devotion, by following the path of grace, one will attain His sacred feet. Therefore, this "maravamai" (not forgetting) refers to not forgetting the sacred feet, the divine grace of God, and offering devotion to Him, not forgetting the help God renders to living beings. Siddhanta explains that even in the state of supreme liberation, the soul will not forget the divine grace of God and the help God renders, and will continue to offer devotion. Thus, he realizes that no matter what state one is in or what guise one adopts, the true essence is never to forget the sacred feet of Sankaran, who possesses enduring glory. "Porul" means truth, knowledge, and benefit. It refers to the liberation, which is the attainment of the soul's ultimate goal. It is understood that true knowledge is to follow His path of grace with tireless love, never forgetting. We saw in the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandar Peruman that he said, "Gnanam enbathu Eesan pal anbu" (Knowledge is love for God). Therefore, our Nayanar considered never forgetting the sacred feet of God as the ultimate truth. Thus, the Nayanar, without abandoning the Sakkiya guise, the Buddhist guise, that he had adopted, excelled in immense love for pure Shiva. "Sakkiyar," that is, the symbols of Buddhists, their attire, are called "tuvara adai" (ochre robes) and "kudai" (umbrella). They also carry a kamandalam (water pot). Without abandoning or discarding such a Buddhist guise, our Nayanar excelled in never forgetting God with immense love for pure Shiva. He firmly stood in never forgetting God, making it his supreme vow, and remained steadfast in his service to God. This Sakkiya Nayanar, even though he did not adopt the Saiva symbols like sacred ash and rudraksha prescribed in the Siva Agamas, and continued to wear the Sakkiya guise, remained steadfast in his service to God with tireless love. "Ayara anbu" means love that is the cause of tirelessness. Sivagnana Bodham states that due to that tireless love, one will reach the Lord's feet. "Ayarama" means not forgetting. Therefore, we can understand that for those who follow the path of intense devotion, knowledge, and detachment, these external guises are not necessary. This rule applies only to Jivanmuktas, who are free from the egoistic sense of "I" and "mine," and not to others. We must very carefully understand and realize this. These are all applicable only to Gnanis (realized souls). The scriptures state that for Gnanis, for Muktas (liberated souls), for those who follow the path of intense devotion, knowledge, and detachment, these guises are not necessary. We must very carefully understand this. Ordinary people should not abandon religious practices by looking at them. They should not deviate from discipline. If we too abandon religious practices by looking at them, we will become fallen and deviate from discipline. We must very carefully keep this in mind. The scriptures state that this rule applies only to Gnanis. Thirumandiram, 1668th mantra: "Gnanamilar vedam poondum naragathar gnanam ullar vedam indrenin nanmuthar gnanam unathaga venduvaar nakkanpal gnanmula veda nanni nirpare." Those who lack divine knowledge, even if they wear the guise of Shiva's devotees, will go to hell. Those who possess divine knowledge, even if they do not wear the guise of Shiva's devotees, are truly liberated. Those who desire to attain divine knowledge will wear the guise that is eternally filled with knowledge, seeking Shiva. This mantra states that wearing a guise always reminds one of God. Therefore, wearing a guise is essential. But if a child wears a king's attire, or a madman wears a minister's attire, there is no benefit for either of them from wearing that attire. However, Gnanis are those who seek Shiva through knowledge and never forget Him. Therefore, for them, wearing guises that remind them of Lord Shiva is not necessary. This is the meaning. We saw that Thirumooladevar Nayanar expressed the same idea in Thirumandiram, which the divine Sekkizhar Peruman also expresses. The 284th Thiruvirutham of Sivagnana Siddhiyar also states this. Those who are in Gnana Nishtha (meditative absorption in knowledge) do not perform any actions. Even if they perform actions, those actions are considered as divine actions, and they do not separate from His golden sacred feet, says this Thiruvirutham. Arulnandi Sivacharyar Peruman states: "Gnanamilarkku nammaiyodu theemai illai. Nadvathu ondru illai. Seelam illai. Thavam illai. Virathamodu achirama seyal illai. Dhyanam illai. Siththa malam illai. Kolam illai. Pulan illai. Karanam illai. Gunam illai. Kuri illai. Kulamum illai. Balarudan unmaththar pisasar gunam maruvi padalinodu adal ivai payindridinum payilvar endru." For those who have attained Gnana Nishtha in this world, there is no good or evil for them. There is nothing to seek. There is no discipline. There is no penance. There are no vows or ashrama activities. There is no meditation. There are no impurities of the mind. There is no external appearance. There are no senses. There are no instruments of knowledge. There are no qualities. There are no goals. There is no caste. These Gnanis, like children, madmen, or those possessed by ghosts, will sing and dance as they please, immersed in supreme bliss. In this Thiruvirutham, Arulnandi Sivacharyar Peruman shows that for the devotees of Shiva, the Sivagnanis, who live in this world as Jivanmuktas and are firmly established in that state, never deviating from contemplation of Shiva even for a moment, there are no specific rules they must follow in their daily lives. He lists, one after another, the Vedic rules they do not need to observe. In the 113th Thiruvirutham of Siddhiyar, it is stated that the rules ordained by God's compassion are: "Ozhukkam, anbu, arul, acharam, upacharam, uravu, seelam, vazhakkila thavam, thanangal, vandhithal, vanangal, vaymai, azhukku ila thuravu, adakkam, arivodu archithal aadhi izhukkila arangal anal iranguvan pani arangal." Here, for those who have attained the state of Jivanmukti and are firmly established in it, never deviating from contemplation of Shiva even for a moment, none of these previously mentioned virtues are necessary. This is because they have already lived by these principles. They are not accustomed to evil conduct. Therefore, all these good qualities naturally reside in them. When they attained the state of Jivanmukti, there were no benefits to be gained from these. Therefore, in life, Jivanmuktas are those who are self-forgetful. The author describes them as "balarudan unmaththar pisasar gunam maruvi" (having the qualities of children, madmen, and those possessed by ghosts). He says they are like children. Children perform some actions. When they do so, they do not consider any benefit. Similarly, those in the state of Jivanmukti perform some actions. But they do not do those actions for their own sake. They do not do them with any benefit in mind. All their actions will be Shiva Punya (meritorious actions for Shiva). Therefore, since their actions are like those of children, he says Jivanmuktas are "balar gunam maruvi" (having the qualities of children). Now, he says the actions of Jivanmuktas are "unmaththar gunangal maruvi" (having the qualities of madmen). A madman is someone who is insane. Therefore, Jivanmuktas also behave like madmen. We see madmen in this world. They do not care about taste, hunger; they run, sing. Sometimes they do one thing, sometimes they don't. They do everything self-forgetfully. They do things without the thought that they are doing them. They babble. They do things as they please, day and night. They don't listen even if others tell them. Similarly, those who have attained the state of Jivanmukti also babble like these madmen. Sometimes they talk to Shiva, cry, laugh, worship, dance. This is the nature of Jivanmuktas. This is what Arulnandi Sivacharyar Peruman shows as "unmaththar gunam maruvi". Now, he says "pisachar gunam maruvi" (having the qualities of ghosts). "Pisachar" means those possessed by ghosts. They perform mischievous acts like hitting and grabbing, as ghosts do. Their behavior is like that of ghosts. Their actions are performed without the sense of "I" in the body. Just as the intellect of those possessed by ghosts is completely occupied by the ghost and their intellect is dulled, these Jivanmuktas also do everything as actions of Lord Shiva, without any egoistic sense of "I". Therefore, Jivanmuktas are those who live in contemplation of Shiva without self-awareness. This is what our ancestors simply say as "siththan pokku Sivan pokku" (the way of the Siddha is the way of Shiva). Furthermore, he says that Jivanmuktas will also "payindridinum payilvar" (practice even singing and dancing). Those who are in contemplation of Shiva, besides meditating on Shiva, also stand as Shiva Himself. Therefore, they sometimes spend their time singing and dancing due to the overflow of supreme bliss. Manikkavasagar Peruman also states this as "oon kettru unarvu kettru ullamum poy nan kettava padi" (My body perished, my consciousness perished, my mind departed, and I perished, singing). Therefore, Arulnandi Sivacharyar Peruman states that Jivanmuktas, no matter what differences in actions they perform, perform them without attachment or aversion, and therefore, without being affected, they constantly contemplate the feet of Shiva. Thirukkural states: "Seyarkariya seyvar periyar, siriyar seyarkariya seykalaathar" (Great ones do what is difficult to do, small ones cannot do what is difficult to do). Here, "periyar" (great ones) refers to Sivagnanis, and "siriyar" (small ones) refers to those who live bound by worldly affairs. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman, in the Puranam of our Sakkiya Nayanar, conveys that Sakkiya Nayanar, no matter what state he was in or what guise he adopted, never forgot the sacred feet of Sankaran, and that he was in such a state even in a Buddhist guise. Therefore, as we have already pondered, this applies only to those who follow the path of intense devotion, knowledge, and detachment, and to those liberated souls who are free from ego, and not to ordinary people. Ordinary people should not abandon religious practices by looking at them. If they do so, they will become fallen and deviate from discipline. We must keep this in mind again and continue to ponder.

Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 05 — Sakkiya Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 919, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Sakkiya Nayanar Puranam. The seventh verse. Those who do not know that the Lord who possesses everything is God, will become Sakkiyas of evil guise and will become base. Those who are not like that, but are able to see that this entire world is His domain, these Nayanars, without changing their guise, stand firm in the path of love. Ishvara is the one who has this entire world as His form. Those who do not realize the truth that Lord Shiva, who is signified by the word Ishvara, is the prime leader, will follow external religions like Buddhism. The word Ishvara, by yogaroodhi (conventional usage based on etymology), denotes Lord Shiva alone. All worlds are His possessions. All living beings are His servants. Those who do not know this will behave basely, thinking that the Sakkiya guise is different from Shiva's form. Sakkiya Nayanar was able to see that this entire world is Shiva's domain and nothing else. Therefore, Sakkiya Nayanar, without changing the guise of a Buddhist, continued to abide in the path of love towards Shiva. The Nayanar, who never forgot the pure Shiva through love and stood firm, understood that the Shiva Lingam is the proper symbol for worshipping that Shiva. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman reveals this in the next verse. The eighth verse. He understood that the Shiva Lingam is the cause of the formless and the formed, which cannot be seen, and is the suitable symbol for the one adorned with a long serpent, and that it appeared as a blazing column of fire in the middle, which neither the tall Vishnu nor the four-faced Brahma could see. The Shiva Lingam is the root and abode for both the formless divine body that cannot be perceived by the eye and the formed divine body that can be perceived by the eye. The Nayanar understood that the Shiva Lingam is the excellent symbol and sign for knowing and worshipping Lord Shiva, who is adorned with a serpent. He understood that the Shiva Lingam is the most excellent form for worshipping Lord Shiva. The Shiva Lingam is said to be the formless-formed divine body of the Lord. It is the Sadashiva divine body. Even if it is said to have taken a form, we have also seen the formless body that had a form. When it became formless-formed, we obtained both divine bodies. "The three mentioned are the form of compassion that came to gladden our dark body," says Sivagnana Siddhiyar. The Shiva Lingam is said to be the formless-formed (aruvuruvam) form. Because a formless, unperceivable entity took the form of a cosmic blazing column, the Shiva Lingam has a form. But since it has no limbs like hands, feet, or face, it is formless. Thus, because both form and formlessness are seen together, it is called aruvuruvam (formless-formed). "Kaanatha aru" means aruvuruvam. The state before seeing is aruvam (formless); from aruvam, aruvuruvam appears, and in that order, from aruvuruvam, uruvam (form) appears. Therefore, the aruvuruvam, which stands in the middle, represents both the formless state above and the formed state below. Thus, the Shiva Lingam is the divine body that stands as the cause for both the unseen formless and the seen formed. The Lord has two states: Thadhastham and Swaroopam. Swaroopam is the inherent nature of the Lord. Vedanta calls it Sat-Chit-Ananda. Siddhanta says He has eight qualities. Thadhastham is the divine body that descends from the Lord's Swaroopa state to bestow grace upon living beings. That Thadhastham comes in three forms: Roopam (formed), Roopa-aroopam (formed-formless), and Aroopam (formless). We saw all this in the commentary on the first verse, "Ulagellam Unarnthu." In Sanskrit, these are also called Sakalam, Nishkalam, and Sakala-Nishkalam. The Shiva Lingam is a means to worship the Lord, who is a great effulgent light beyond qualities and signs, by placing Him in a symbol. The Shiva Lingam is extensively discussed in the Vedas, Agamas, Itihasas, Puranas,

Thirumurais, and Shastras. This Shiva Lingam appeared as a blazing column of fire between Brahma and Vishnu. Therefore, the Nayanar, who never forgot the pure Shiva through love and stood firm, understood that the Shiva Lingam is the proper symbol for worshipping that Shiva. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman describes in the next verse how the Nayanar, having understood this, worshipped it. Every day, desiring to see and contemplate the Shiva Lingam, he saw a Shiva Lingam established in a nearby open space, and with his mind filled with overflowing joy, he became unaware of what he was doing, and picking up a stone nearby, he threw it with the agitation born of love, as if it were a flower. Sakkiya Nayanar wished to adopt the discipline of eating only after seeing and worshipping the Shiva Lingam every day. He saw a Shiva Lingam established in a nearby open space, and his mind was filled with an overflowing joy that lasted long. Upon seeing it, he reached a state of ecstasy and, unaware of what he was doing, picked up a stone nearby and threw it at the Shiva Lingam, considering it a flower, with the agitation born of love. There are many types of Shiva worship. The Nayanar adopted one of them: eating only after seeing the Shiva Lingam. Therefore, we are going to see that this Nayanar attained liberation by adhering to the discipline of seeing the Shiva Lingam every day before eating, worshipping it as best as he could, and then eating, never forgetting this practice. Thus, the special nature of the Shiva Lingam worship as a discipline is revealed. Because of the firmness of this discipline in his mind, the Nayanar, while in that state, saw a Shiva Lingam in an open space nearby. As soon as he saw it, a long-lasting great joy arose within him. Unaware of what he was doing due to that great joy, he picked up a stone nearby and threw it at the Lord's divine body, considering it a flower. Our Nayanar did this, forgetting that throwing stones at the Lord's divine body is one of the forbidden sins. The Nayanar did this as soon as he saw the Shiva Lingam standing in an open space. The Shiva Lingam was in an open space, not covered by a temple building or anything else. Many such Shiva Lingams can be found in open spaces in Kanchipuram and other holy places. These indicate the lack of good awareness among Saivites. The Lord being in an open space does not benefit Him in any way. But leaving these Shiva Lingams neglected in such a way causes distress to the people who see Shiva Lingams in open spaces without temples. It is a rule of the Shiva Agamas that the Lord's divine bodies should be protected by providing shade and proper care. Therefore, it is emphasized here that all Saivite devotees should not leave Shiva Lingams in open spaces but should make proper arrangements for their worship. The Nayanar saw a stone, picked it up, and threw it. He considered that object, which causes pain, as something that gives pleasure and threw it. He threw it as if he were offering flowers. That itself became an archana (offering). He threw the stone as if he were offering it. This is the meaning of the ninth verse. Now, the tenth verse. With overflowing joy and boundless love, parents rejoice and are delighted by their children. Even if they do things that seem harsh, it brings joy to their young children. Similarly, the Lord with the long matted locks rejoices in that. The Nayanar did this out of the boundless love that arose from his overflowing joy. How did the Lord accept this? Just as parents, seeing their children, feel great love and joy for them, and when that joy overflows, they sometimes do harsh things like gently hitting, pinching, or holding their children tightly.

They do these harsh actions out of immense love and joy for their children. The children also understand this. They realize that their parents are pinching, hitting, or embracing them tightly out of great joy and love, and they express their joy accordingly. We see in the world that even such harsh actions can bring joy. Generally, violent actions like hitting and pinching are not allowed, but in this situation, even these harsh actions, done out of great joy, result in pleasure. Similarly, the Lord with the long matted locks rejoiced in this harsh act of throwing stones performed by Sakkiya Nayanar. He became filled with joy. The 11th verse. The next day, when he was about to eat, he remembered the act of throwing a stone at the Lord with the Konrai flower on His head. He realized that it was the Lord's grace that made him do it, and he resolved to continue that as his service forever. The event we saw above happened for the first time on the first day. After that, on the second day, when the Nayanar went for the darshan of the Shiva Lingam, according to his vow of eating only after seeing the Shiva Lingam, he remembered the act of throwing a stone at the Lord's divine body on the previous day. What did he realize? He realized that it was the Lord's grace that caused that thought to occur to him at that time, and he resolved to continue that as his service every day from then on. He continued to do so. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 06 — Sakkiya Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 920, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Sakkiya Nayanar Puranam. Sakkiya Nayanar made it a regular practice to throw stones at the Shiva Lingam as an offering. The Lord, from that Shiva Lingam, graciously accepted that service with great joy. How can one describe the greatness of the Shiva Lingam, which stands as a suitable symbol for the Lord adorned with a long serpent, the cause of both the unseen formless and the seen formed? We saw that the divine Sekkizhar Peruman illustrates the significance of the Shiva Lingam here. That verse in Periyapuranam explains the philosophical meaning of the Shiva Lingam. Based on that verse, we should briefly contemplate the greatness of the Shiva Lingam. Shiva Lingam means "Shiva as Lingam." In Shiva Lingam, "Shi" is said to denote Shiva as Nada (sound/consciousness), and "Va" is said to denote Shakti as Peetam (base), thus referring to the Lingam associated with Nada and Bindu. That is the meaning of Shiva Lingam. The Skanda Purana states, "Shivaiyeva Swayam Lingam" (Shiva Himself is the Lingam). The Agama states, "Nadam Lingam Itiknyeyam Bindu Peetamudakrutam" (Nada is known as Lingam, Bindu is said to be the Peetam). This matter of the Shiva Lingam, this greatness of the Shiva Lingam, is expounded in the Vedas, prescribed in the Agamas, narrated in the Puranas, described in the Itihasas, and mentioned in the Smritis. Many great souls have worshipped it and attained great blessings. In the fifth Mandala, third Sukta, third Mantra of the Rig Veda, it is found: "Tavasriye Maruto Marjayanta Rudrayatte Janima Charuchitram." This mantra states, "O Rudra, the Maruts, to attain your wealth, performed abhisheka and other pujas to your wonderful form, the Mahalingam, which is the divine body of Shiva." Great scholars interpret "Janima" in this mantra as Lingam. The Parasara Upabrumhanam states: "Roudram Lingam Mahavishnur Bhaktiya Suddham Silamayam Charuchitram Samarpayasya Labdhavaan Paramam Padam." It states that Lord Mahavishnu, with devotion, worshipped the pure, stone-made, beautiful and wonderful Shiva Lingam and attained the supreme abode of Vaikuntha. The Rig Veda states "Charuchitram Te Janima," and the Parasara Purana interprets it as "Charuchitram Roudram Lingam." It is said that the Vedas should be interpreted with the help of Itihasas and Puranas. The Bharata Itihasa Purana statements say that the Vedas fear that a person with shallow understanding, who does not interpret them this way, will interpret the Vedas as he pleases and harm them. Therefore, the Parasara Purana interprets "Charuchitram Te Janima" from the Rig Veda as "Charuchitram Roudram Lingam." Thus, the Vedic statement "Roudram Janima" refers to the Shiva Lingam. Interpreting the Vedas with the help of Upabrumhanam is the true commentary. Our tradition holds that commentators who do not understand this will write different meanings and be at fault. The Viruddhagiri Mahatmyam also states that Vishnu, in the beginning, worshipped the beautiful stone Shiva Lingam with love and attained His rare, primeval, supreme, and excellent position. In the 10th Prashna, 16th Anuvaka of the Yajur Veda Taittiriya Aranyaka, it is stated: "Shivalingaya Namaha" (Salutations to the Shiva Lingam). Researchers inquire about the age of Shiva Lingam worship. Our tradition is to consider that Shiva Lingam worship has existed as long as the Vedas have existed. The Vedas are the eternal words of the Lord, so we must consider them to be eternal. If we look at the meaning of the root word "Lingam," it is said: "Layam Gachchanti Bhutani Samhare Nikhilani Cha Srushtikale Yatha Srushtis Tasma Lingamudakrutam." This is a divine saying. The Suprabheda Agama states that because all conscious and unconscious universes merge during dissolution and then are created again during creation, it is called Lingam. Therefore, the meaning of the root word "Lingam" is established by the Suprabheda Agama as that which serves as the place for all conscious and unconscious universes to merge and then be created. We see this phenomenon occurring in every unconscious and conscious object in the world. That is, we see dissolution and creation occurring in every unconscious and conscious object in this world. Trees grow from seeds. Those trees grow tall, become stout, bud, flower, bear fruit, then wither, struggle, and when they perish, the seeds of those trees remain dormant in the soil for some time in a subtle form and then sprout into saplings and become trees. Now, the water in the ocean, atom by atom, rises due to the sun's heat, goes into the atmosphere, becomes clouds, then reaches the mountains, rains down, and those rains become small springs, then streams, then rivers, and finally merge into the ocean. When it rains in the mountains, the mountain streams gradually erode and wear down the mountains, carrying fine sand to one place, which then coalesce into stones and gradually rise to form mountains. These are some examples of how dissolution and creation continuously occur in unconscious objects. Now, if we look at conscious objects, birds lay eggs and die, and then chicks emerge from those eggs and grow into adults. Animals give birth to calves, cubs, young ones, and then die, and those young ones grow into adults. We see that all effects are in gross form, and all causes are in subtle form. Just as the power of the Lingam is seen to appear and disappear in the animate and inanimate objects we observe daily, so too the earth, stars, sun, moon, and others will one day merge and reappear, and that is determined to be the Shiva Lingam. Now let's see how the Lingam explains the Panchakrityam (five divine functions). Objects that appear do not immediately perish but remain for some time and then perish. In each, three functions occur: creation, sustenance, and dissolution. Great scholars say that among the three functions of creation, sustenance, and dissolution that occur during the great creation, the function of sustenance includes the function of concealment (tirobhava), and the function of dissolution includes the function of grace (anugraha). Therefore, there are five functions in total, called Panchakrityam. These are sometimes grouped into three. It is understood that the Panchakrityam, namely creation, sustenance, dissolution, concealment, and grace, is depicted in the three types of universes: the impure universe belonging to the Atma Tattvas, the pure-impure universe belonging to the Vidya Tattvas, and the pure universe belonging to the Shiva Tattvas. Among these, creation, sustenance, and dissolution are performed by the Lord in Maya. Concealment and grace are performed by the Lord in living beings. The Varna Paddhati states that since the word "Lingam" is derived from the root "Liki," which means to depict, it is understood that Lord Shiva depicts the universe through the Panchakrityam, such as creation. The Varna Paddhati states: "Lingam Shabdena Attra Linga Chitrekarana Dhatvartha Katya Linga Iti Srushti Sthityadibhihi Krityair Jagat Chitrekarana Karoteeti Parameshvaraha." Thus, the word "Lingam," which explains the power of the Panchakrityam, is also metaphorically applied to idols and other objects. The Shiva Agamas state: "Nadam Lingam Smritam," meaning that the upper part of the Lingam, Nada, is called Lingam, and the lower part, Bindu, is called Peetam. Thus, this Lingam, which is in the form of Shiva-Shakti, signifies that the entire universe is Shiva and Shakti. There is an Upanishad called Rudra Hrudaya Upanishad. It offers salutations to the Lord as follows: "Rudro Lingam Uma Peetam Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudro Nara Uma Nari Tasmai Tasyai Namaha. Rudro Brahma Uma Vani Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudro Vishnu Uma Lakshmi Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudraha Surya Uma Chaya Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudraha Soma Uma Tara Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudro Diva Uma Ratris Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudro Yagna Uma Vedi Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudro Agni Uma Swaha Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudro Veda Uma Shastram Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudro Vriksha Uma Valli Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha. Rudro Gandha Uma Pushpam Tasmai Tasyai Namo Namaha." Salutations to both Rudra, who is the Lingam, and Uma, who is the Peetam. Salutations to Lord Shiva and Goddess Uma, who are respectively man and woman, Brahma and Saraswati, Vishnu and Lakshmi, Sun and Chaya, Moon and Tara, day and night, Yagna and Vedi, Agni and Swaha, Veda and Shastra, tree and creeper, mind and flower. The Rudra Hrudaya Upanishad states this. Just as there is no child without parents, there would be no universe without Shiva and Shakti. Shiva is the father, Shakti is the mother. Shiva is the Paramatma, Shakti is Manonmani. Shiva is Maheswaran, Shakti is Maya. Shiva is Purusha, Shakti is Prakriti. Shiva is Rudra, Shakti is Rudrani. Shiva is Vishnu, Uma is Lakshmi. Shiva is Brahma, Shakti is Saraswati. Shiva is Surya, Shakti is Prabha. Shiva is Indra, Shakti is Indrani. Shiva is Agni, Shakti is Swaha Devi. Shiva is Yama, Shakti is Yami. Shiva is Rudhi, Shakti is Nirudhi. Shiva is Varuna, Shakti is Gauri. Shiva is Vayu, Shakti is Sivai. Shiva is Kubera, Shakti is Vriddhi. Shiva is Chandra, Shakti is Rohini. Shiva is Ishana, Shakti is Ishani. Shiva is Ananta, Shakti is Anantai. Shiva is Kalagni Rudra, Shakti is Kali. Shiva is Swayambhuva Manu, Shakti is Satarupa. Shiva is Daksha, Shakti is Prasuti. Shiva is Ruchi, Shakti is Ahuti. Shiva is Bhrigu, Shakti is Khyati. Shiva is Marichi, Shakti is Sambhuti. Shiva is Angiras, Shakti is Smriti. Shiva is Pulastya, Shakti is Preeti. Shiva is Pulaha, Shakti is Kshama. Shiva is Kratu, Shakti is Sannidhi. Shiva is Atri, Shakti is Anasuya. Shiva is Kashyapa, Shakti is Aditi. Shiva is Vasishtha, Shakti is Arundhati. Thus, all male forms are Shiva, all female forms are Shakti. All objects are Shakti, and those who experience them are Shiva. The one who hears, touches, and sees is Shiva. That which is heard, touched, and seen is Shakti. Tastes are Shakti, and the one who tastes is Shiva. The mind is Shakti, and the one who experiences them is Shiva. Life is Shiva, the body is Shakti. Day is Shiva, night is Shakti. Sky is Shiva, earth is Shakti. Ocean is Shiva, its shore is Shakti. All words are Shakti, and their meanings are Shiva. Thus states the fifth chapter of the Uttara Bhaga of Vayu Samhita. Thus, Lord Shiva and Shakti, inseparable, reveal the glory of the Shiva Lingam. Therefore, this entire universe is in the form of Shiva, in the form of the Shiva Lingam. Is there anywhere in this universe the mark of Indra's Vajrayudha?

Is there a symbol? No. Is there Brahma's lotus symbol? Is there Vishnu's chakra symbol? None are seen. Only the Linga and Bhaga (male and female) aspects of Shiva and Shakti are real. Therefore, the entire universe is in the form of Shiva and Shakti, says Gnana Siddhagama. The Gnanaavarana Vilakkam also states this truth. "There are no fish-marks, flower-marks, or vajra-marks on this earth, O madmen! All are Linga and Bhaga aspects; there is nothing other than our Lord." Thus states Gnanaavarana Vilakkam. It is essential to worship Lord Shiva by establishing Him in the symbol of the Lingam. The Koyil Puranam states: "Though the King, beyond speech and mind, is omnipresent and fills everything without separation, yet for the sake of attaining liberation, the Holy One, realizing that it would not be easy, out of His compassion, placed countless holy places on earth for our good fortune." The Koyil Puranam states that Lord Shiva, who transcends speech and mind, is omnipresent like fire in wood, oil in sesame, and milk in a cow, yet this truth is not apparent to everyone. Therefore, Lord Shiva Himself, realizing that attaining liberation would not be easy, and out of His compassion, placed countless holy places on earth for our good fortune. The human mind naturally desires to go after external things. Therefore, a form is necessary to turn the mind away from those external things. This idol worship is of two types: internal and external. One is worshipping idols and the like. The other is worshipping mentally. If we carefully examine other religions that say idol worship is not allowed, we will easily find that they too, in some way, worship an idol or a symbol. Therefore, worshipping in an idol is one way, but worshipping in a Lingam is considered superior by the Vedas and all other sacred texts. Let us continue to contemplate the greatness of the Shiva Lingam. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 07 — Sakkiya Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 921, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Sakkiya Nayanar Puranam. We have been contemplating the greatness of the Shiva Lingam. We saw that Shiva Lingam worship is superior and more excellent than idol worship. The Mahabharata contains an incident that illustrates this. In the Mahabharata war, Ashwatthama used the Agneyastra against Arjuna and Krishna, who are considered Nara and Narayana. That Agneyastra incinerated all the Pandava armies, but Arjuna and Krishna remained unburnt. Arjuna immediately launched the Pasupatastra. As soon as it was launched, the Agneyastra lost its power and subsided. Everyone understood that this was why Krishna and Arjuna escaped the Agneyastra. Ashwatthama, seeing his arrow lose its power, became sorrowful, threw down his bow, dismounted his chariot, and fled without fighting. Sage Vyasa met him and asked Ashwatthama why he was fleeing. Ashwatthama replied, "No one, be they Asuras, Gandharvas, Pishachas, Rakshasas, Uragas, Yakshas, birds, or humans, can withstand the Agni arrow I unleashed. That Agni arrow only defeated the Pandava armies, but it did not defeat Krishna and Arjuna. It lost its power to defeat them. Why is that? I wish to know the reason." Sage Vyasa then said to Ashwatthama, "Narayana performed penance for 66,000 years in the Himalayas, meditating on Lord Shiva, and received great boons through the grace of Lord Shiva. Arjuna, who is Nara, also appeared from Narayana's penance. Not only that, you are also great in birth, karma, and penance, like Kesava and Arjuna. But the difference between you and them is that in every yuga, those two, Nara and Narayana, worship Lord Shiva in the Shiva Lingam. You worship Lord Shiva in an idol. Kesava, who emerged from Rudra, is a devotee of Shiva. Narayana, knowing the all-pervading nature of Lord Shiva, worships the Lord Shiva Lingam. Therefore, Lord Shiva, who has the bull banner, is greatly pleased with this Krishna." This is stated in the Drona Parva as spoken by Sage Vyasa. The Mahabharata sloka states: "Tabhyam Lingochito Devas Tvaya Archayam Yuge Yuge Sa Esha Rudra Bhaktasya Keshavo Rudra Sambhava Sarva Roopam Bhavam Gnatva Lingam Archayati Prabhum Tasmin Napyadhiko Preetim Karoti Vrishabhadhwajaha." Therefore, the Shiva Lingam is superior to all Maheswara forms, which is a truth stated in the Shiva Agamas. Thus, one should understand that worshipping in a Shiva Lingam is far more special than worshipping in idols. When Lord Shiva performs the eternal creation for the souls in the eternal state of isolation, He Himself takes the form of a holy place, a holy water body, and an idol, and resides there. Therefore, through residence in holy places, touching holy waters, and worshipping the Shiva Lingam, Lord Shiva bestows many kinds of benefits and Shiva merits upon a person. Regarding this, Tayumanavar Swamigal also states, "For those who begin in the order of Murthi, Sthalam, Theertham, a good Guru will also speak a word, O Paraparam." The Shankara Samhita states that among gems, mantras, and medicines, the Shiva Lingam is the gem. It states: "Shivalingam Maniki Saksham Mantrak Panchaksharastada Bhutire Aushadham Pumsam." Among gems, mantras, and medicines, for humans, the Shiva Lingam is the gem. The mantra is the Panchakshara.

The Vibhuti is the medicine, says the Shankara Samhita. Generally, "mani" in the worldly sense refers to a mercury pill. It is said that Siddhas perform aerial travel and other feats with this pill. Through Shiva Lingam worship, one can attain both worldly and otherworldly siddhis (perfections). Now, let's discuss the types of Lingams. The Shiva Lingam is of three types: Avyakta Lingam (unmanifest), Vyakta-avyakta Lingam (manifest-unmanifest), and Vyakta Lingam (manifest). The Avyakta Lingam is one with a pedestal and a Lingam. The Vyakta-avyakta Lingam is one where the face and shoulders are visible on the Lingam. The 25 Maheswara forms, such as Chandrashekhara and Somasakanda, where all limbs are visible, are Vyakta Lingams. Furthermore, the Shiva Lingam is of four types: Achala Lingam (immovable), Chala Lingam (movable), Chala-achala Lingam (movable-immovable), and Achala-chala Lingam (immovable-movable). Gopurams (temple towers) and Vimanas (temple structures) are considered Achala Lingams. The Shiva Lingam conceived in the heart is a Chala Lingam. Bana Lingams, Ratna Lingams, etc., are Chala-achala Lingams. If a Lingam is consecrated and worshipped in a Vedic altar, it is an Achala-chala Lingam. Bana Lingams and others are considered Ishta Lingams (personal worship). If worshipped as an Anga Lingam (part of the body) in the presence of Shiva during Parartha Puja (worship for others), it is a Chala Lingam. If established for Parartha, it is an Achala Lingam, hence it is called Chala-achala. If consecrated and worshipped in a Vedic altar as prescribed for initiation, it is an Achala Lingam. If worshipped daily as an Ishta Lingam, it is a Chala Lingam, hence it is called Achala-chala. The Stupi (finial) is a Sthula Lingam (gross Lingam). The Shiva Lingam enshrined in the Vimana (sanctum) is a Sukshma Lingam (subtle Lingam), meaning the Shiva Lingam enshrined in the Garbhagriha (sanctum sanctorum) is a Sukshma Lingam, and the Bali Peetam (altar) in front of it is a Bhadra Lingam. This truth is revealed by Thirumooladeva Nayanar in Thirumantiram: "The pure Vimana is the gross, the good Sadashiva is the subtle, the widespread Bali Peetam is the Bhadra Lingam, those who understand will realize the divine state." The Basma Jabala Upanishad states that the Shiva Lingam should be worshipped three times a day: morning, noon, and evening. The Katha Upanishad states that the Purusha, of the size of a thumb, resides in the center of the heart. He is the Lord of past and future, and therefore fears no one. The one described as thumb-sized is Lord Shiva Himself. This is evident from the word "Ishana" which is a conventional term found in the Katha Upanishad mantra. Based on the meaning of this Katha Upanishad, which states that Lord Shiva, for the sake of His devotees, stands in the center of their hearts in the form of a Lingam, Sage Vyasa composed a Sutra in the Vedanta Sutras, interpreting the Shruti "Saptadeva Pramitaka" as "measured by the thumb-size." Appayya Dikshitar, having thoroughly researched and understood this, also stated and established the same in his Shiva Tattva Viveka. As seen in the Shruti, there are two types of Lingams: Sthavara Lingam (immovable) and Jangama Lingam (movable). The Shiva Agamas state that the Sthavara Lingam is the Shiva Lingam enshrined in a temple, and the Jangama Lingam is the Lingam in the hearts of devotees. Lingams are of two kinds: Chala Lingam and Achala Lingam. The Shiva Agama states that if the Jangama Lingam, which is a Chala Lingam, is disrespected, then the Sthavara Lingam, which is an Achala Lingam, will be of no use. This is why Sivagnana Siddhiyar states, "Those who have no love for Ishan have no love for His devotees." Now, there are many types of Lingams used in external worship. A baked clay Lingam is 10 times more potent than a green clay Lingam. A wooden Lingam is 10 times more potent than that. A stone Lingam is 10 times more potent than that. A metallic Lingam is 100 times more potent than that. A gem Lingam is infinitely more potent than that. A Mani Lingam (gem-like Lingam) is a lakh times more potent than that. A Bana Lingam is a crore times more potent than that. A Rasa Lingam (mercury Lingam) is a crore times more potent than that. Even more potent than that is a Swayambhu Lingam (self-manifested Lingam). One Shiva Nabha (a type of fossilized shell) is equal to 1000 Salagramas. One crystal Lingam is equal to 1000 Shiva Nabhas. One Bana Lingam is equal to 12 lakh crystal Lingams. The Lingams found in the Bana river, from the first cubit to the last, were worshipped with mantras by Banasura, and hence they are called Bana Lingams. No invocation is needed for these Lingams. Shiva's presence is always there. The places worshipped by Banasura are Lingadri, Kalikartham, Srigiri, Kanikasramam, Kannathirtham, Nepal, Mahendragiri, and Amareswaram. Brahma, Vishnu, Devas, and Asuras established and worshipped Lingams found in sacred rivers like Narmada, Ganga, and Yamuna for the benefit of others. The weapons of those who worshipped them will be seen as symbols on the Shiva Lingams they worshipped. Now, Lingams are of seven types: Swayambhu Lingam, Devi Lingam, Divya Lingam, Arshaka Lingam, Manusha Lingam, Rakshasa Lingam, and Asura Lingam. A Lingam that appeared on its own is a Swayambhu Lingam. A Lingam worshipped by the Goddess is a Devi Lingam. A Lingam established by the Devas is a Daivika Lingam. A Lingam worshipped by the Rishis is an Arshaka Lingam. A Lingam worshipped by the Rakshasas is a Rakshasa Lingam. A Lingam established by the Asuras is an Asura Lingam. A Bana Lingam is one that originated in water (Bana means water). The Bana Lingam is the one worshipped by Banasura. By implication, Lingams established by Ganesha and Subrahmanya are called Ganalingams. Now, let's look at examples of the above-mentioned Lingams. The large, mountainous Thirumoolasthana Shiva Lingam in Chidambaram is a Swayambhu Lingam. The Ekambaranatha Mahalingam worshipped by Kamakshi Amman in Kanchi Ekambam is a Devi Lingam. The Ganapatheeswara Mahalingam established and worshipped by Ganesha in Thiruchengattangudi, and the Shaktigireeswara Mahalingam established and worshipped by Subrahmanya in Thiruchengallur, are revered as Ganalingams. The Brahmapureeswara Mahalingam established and worshipped by Brahma in Sirkazhi, the Malvanangeeswara Mahalingam worshipped by Vishnu in Thirumarpettru, the Manikavartheeswara Mahalingam established and worshipped by Vishnu in His Vamana avatar in Thirumanikuzhi, and the Kannayireeswara Mahalingam established and worshipped by Indra in Thirukannanaar Kovil, all belong to the Daivika Lingam category. The Kurumbalaveeswara Mahalingam established and worshipped by Agastya in Thirukutralam is an Arusha Lingam. The Sargunatheeswara Mahalingam established and worshipped by Idumba in Idumbavanam is a Rakshasa Lingam. The Onakantheeswara Mahalingam worshipped by the two Asuras, Onan and Kandan, in Thiruvonakanthan Thali, is an Asura Lingam. Now, the Rudrakoti Samhita mentions the twelve Jyotirlingams. The Somnath Lingam in Saurashtra, the Mallikarjuna Lingam in Srisailam, the Mahakala Lingam in Ujjain, the Parameswara Lingam in Omkareshwar, the Kedara Lingam in the Himalayas, the Bhimashankara Lingam in Dakini, the Visweswara Lingam in Varanasi, the Trayambaka Lingam in Godavari, the Vaidyanatha Lingam in Chitaburam, the Nageswara Lingam in Darukavanam, the Rameswara Lingam in Sethu, and the Kusumesha Lingam are the 12 Jyotirlingams, says the Kotirudra Samhita. It also states that those who recite the names of these 12 Jyotirlingams in the morning will be freed from all sins and attain all siddhis, and that anyone can worship these Lingams. Besides these Dvadasha Lingams, there are five Panchabhuta Mahalingams that can remove sins by mere thought. These are the Prithvi-maya Ekambara Lingam in Kanchipuram, the Appu-maya Jambukeswara Lingam on the banks of the Kaveri, the Agni-maya Arunachala Lingam in Arunachalam, the Vayu-maya Sri Kalahasti Lingam on the Ponmukali river, and the Akasha-maya Thirumoola Lingam in Chidambaram. The Kotirudra Samhita states that by merely seeing, hearing, speaking, or meditating on these five Lingams, one becomes pure and attains desired perfections. Furthermore, there are Upalingams for those 12 Jyotirlingams. If all of them were to be mentioned here, it would become too extensive. Why so much? There is no count for Shiva Lingams. The earth itself is full of Lingams. The entire universe is full of Lingams. All holy waters like the Ganga are full of Lingams. The entire world is contained within the Lingam. Just as on earth, there are Lingams in the netherworld.

and in heaven. Humans on earth, Nagas in the netherworld, and Devas and Rakshasas in heaven, all perform Shiva Lingam puja. Lord Shiva manifests in the form of the Lingam throughout the animate and inanimate universe to bestow grace upon souls. He appears in those places and grants the desired perfections to all who worship Him with love. Now, the six Adhara Sthalams, also called Kshetrams, which are the six energy centers. This entire universe is the body of Brahma. The seven lower worlds are below the waist, namely the car, big toe, back of the foot, instep, ankle, knee, middle of the thigh, and root of the thigh. The seven upper worlds are above the waist, namely the seven worlds like Bhumi (earth) in the Muladhara and so on. Brahma's Muladhara is Kamalalayam. Here, Lord Shiva is called Thyagesar. The Lingasthanam, which is Swadhishtana, is Thiruvanaikka. There, He is called Jambunathar. The Nabhi Sthanam, which is Manipooraka, is Arunachalam. There, He is called Arunachaleswarar. The Hrudaya Sthanam, which is Anahata, is where He is called Sabhapathi. The Kanta Sthanam, which is Vishuddhi, is Sri Kalahasti. There, He is called Kalahasteeswarar. The Bhruva Madhyasthanam, which is Ajna, is Kasi. There, He is called Viswanathar. The Brahmarandhram is Thirukailasam. There, He is called Srikanta Parameswarar. The Dwadasanta, which is beyond the Adharas, is Madurai. There, He is called Sundareswarar, says the Agastya Samhita. Now, the Koyil Puranam speaks about the places that grant liberation upon birth or death. Being born in Thiruvarur or dying in Kasi grants the bliss of liberation after a long time. Thillai Vanam is a place of Jnana Yoga. Those who behold the sacred feet there can roam as Jivanmuktas, says the Koyil Puranam. Now, the Shiva Agamas describe the methods of worship for those who follow the paths of Charya, Kriya, Yoga, and Jnana, and how the Lord bestows grace upon them. Those who worship the Shiva Lingam enshrined in a temple as Lord Shiva Himself are followers of the Charya path and other paths. For followers of the Charya path, Lord Shiva manifests implicitly, not explicitly. Those who worship by performing Mantra Nyasa, believing that Lord Shiva, who is the Nishkala (formless) being, took the form of the Shiva Lingam and other idols through Mantras like Ishana, are followers of the Kriya path. For them, Lord Shiva, like fire appearing when churned, manifests in the desired form in those idols at those times, according to the respective Mantras, and bestows grace. Now, those who follow the Yoga path worship by believing that Lord Shiva, who pervades their hearts, also resides in the Shiva Lingam and other forms, and accepts worship through Sadhya Mantras. For them, Lord Shiva, like milk appearing immediately after milking, manifests in the desired form in those idols at those times, according to the respective Mantras, and bestows grace. Now, those who follow the Jnana path worship with love alone. For them, Lord Shiva, like the milk from the udder of a cow that remembers its calf, manifests eternally and without separation, out of abundant compassion, and bestows grace. This truth is stated in Sivagnana Siddhiyar. Sivagnana Siddhiyar explains this in the Thiruvirutham that begins with "Thirukoyil Ullirukkum Thirumeni Thannai." Among all pujas, Shiva Lingam puja is the best. The Manu Smriti states that Brahmins, who are devoted to the five yagnas, should worship the five deities: Aditya, Ambika, Vishnu, Ganesha, and Mahadeva. So, if the question arises whether these five should be worshipped equally or if one of them should be worshipped as superior to all others, then according to the rule that doubts arising in Smritis should be resolved by the Moola Pramana (root authority) of the Vedas, we can find the answer to this question in the Upanishads, which are part of the Vedas. If we look at the Svetasvatara Upanishad, it states that there is no one equal to or greater than Lord Shiva. The Veda praises Vishnu, Brahma, and others. Does it praise anyone else as much as it praises Lord Shiva? The Vedas praise other deities merely as a formality. The Atharva Sikha Upanishad, which is the crest jewel of all Upanishads, which are the heads of the Vedas, states the following: "Meditating on Ishana, who is most worthy of meditation, Brahma, Vishnu, Rudra, and Indra are born. All senses and elements are born with them. The cause and the creators of causes and those who conceived them are not born. The cause, the one endowed with all prosperity, the Lord of all, Shambhu, is the one to be meditated upon in the sky. He is the one to be meditated upon." Thus, the Atharva Sikha Upanishad explains other deities. When the Atharva Sikha Upanishad concludes, it states, "Shiva Eko Deyaka," meaning that Lord Shiva alone, who bestows happiness, is to be meditated upon. Thus, in its final part, this Upanishad excludes everyone else and states that Lord Shiva alone, among the Panchamurtis, is worthy of worship and should be worshipped. Now, let's contemplate who worshipped the Shiva Lingam and what benefits they received. Vishnu performed penance for 10,000 years in Kasi Kshetra, worshipped the Shiva Lingam, and was crowned to attain His supreme position and the right to protect all worlds. Brahma went to Kanchipuram, worshipped the Shiva Lingam, and obtained the authority to create, the status of consort to Saraswati, and the swan as His vehicle. Similarly, Indra, Agni, Yama, Niruti, Varuna, Vayu, Kubera, and Ishana, the eight Dikpalakas (guardians of directions), worshipped the Shiva Lingam in Srisailam, Thiruvannamalai, Kedaram, Gokarnam, Jambukeswaram, Thirukalahasti, Siddhavatam, and Thiruvarur respectively, and obtained their respective directional suzerainties, vehicles, and weapons from Lord Shiva, and lived gloriously. Not just the Dikpalakas? Due to the special power of Shiva Lingam puja, the Sun and Moon circumambulate Mount Meru, the abode of Lord Shiva. Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn worshipped the Shiva Lingam in Thiruvidaimarudur, Madurai, Sethu, Thiruvengadu, and Vedaranyam respectively, and obtained their desired boons, were freed from suffering, and live happily. The seven Rishis worshipped Sabhanayaka in Chidambaram and obtained their abode, the Saptarishi Mandala, above all planets, and received the grace to attain liberation in the human body. Dhruva worshipped Lord Kasi Viswanatha and stands like a pivot above the Navagraha Mandala, Nakshatra Mandala, and Saptarishi Mandala, making them revolve. Adisesha worshipped Lord Shiva in Kumbakonam and bears the burden of the earth. Thus, in all places like earth, mountain peaks, and riverbanks, Devas, Rishis, Asuras, Siddhas, Garudas, Yakshas, Vidyadharas, Mahoragas, Kinnaras, great sages, Kimpurushas, and humans, all worship the Shiva Lingam and attain their respective positions and live happily. Some are rulers of clouds, some are Dik Gajas (directional elephants), some are Ashtama Nagas (eight great serpents), and some are the seven Kulaparvatas (main mountains). Whoever is the lord of whomever, they have all obtained those positions through the grace of Lord Shiva. Due to the power of Shiva Lingam puja, some became Siddhas. Some became the nine Siddha Yogis like Kapila. If Lord Shiva accepts the Bilva leaves thrown by a blind woman on Shivaratri day in Gokarnam, and the Bilva leaves dropped one by one by a hunter from a Bilva tree for staying awake all night, and bestows grace upon them, then can one measure His compassion? Due to the power of Shiva Lingam puja, some have attained Shiva Salokyam, some Shiva Samipyam, Shiva Sarupyam, and Shiva Sayujyam. Due to the power of Shiva Lingam puja, Devas, Asuras, and humans live with great prosperity. Many who desired victory worshipped the Shiva Lingam, defeated their enemies, and ruled the world. Some obtained desired children, wives, cows, horses, beauty, generosity, truth, and valor. If one starts narrating the histories of how Brahma, Vishnu, and others worshipped Lord Shiva at different times for different reasons and received different boons, they would become very extensive. Now, regarding Parartha Lingam, the Karana Agama states that a Lingam specially established according to rules in villages and other places by Devas, Rishis, and humans for the sake of bestowing grace upon all souls in the world is called a Parartha Lingam. And an Ishta Lingam, which everyone worships in their own home for the protection of their soul, is called an Atmartha Lingam. Thus, the Karana Agama speaks about Atmartha Lingam and Parartha Lingam. The explanation of Atmartha Lingam is as follows: When the Acharya performs a special initiation and instructs the disciple, "Worship this daily without fail as long as you live," and the initiated one vows, "I will not consume anything without performing Shiva Puja as long as my body exists," and the Acharya gives it and the disciple receives and worships it, that Lingam is called an Ishta Lingam or Atmartha Lingam. Ishta Lingams are of six types: Shaila Lingam (stone), Ratna Lingam (gem), Lokaja Lingam (metal), Daruja Lingam (wood), Mrinmaya Lingam (clay), and Kshanika Lingam (temporary). Brahmins and the other four varnas, and the six Anulomas (children of higher caste fathers and lower caste mothers), making ten castes in total, who are without physical defects, are eligible for Ishta Lingam puja. Among these, only those who are free from illness, have the necessary resources, servants, and knowledge of Shiva Puja rules and expiatory rites, and are capable of performing them as known, can have Shaila and other Shiva Lingams consecrated and perform Shiva Puja. All others should perform Kshanika Lingam puja. The methods of examining Shiva Lingams are extensively described in the Shiva Agamas. A Kshanika Lingam is a Lingam that is discarded after worship. Kshanika Lingams are of twelve types: sand, rice, cooked rice, river clay, cow dung, butter, Rudraksha, sacred ash, sandalwood paste, Kurcha (grass bundle), flower garland, and sugar. It was stated above that the Shiva Lingam is in the form of a pedestal and a Lingam. This Shiva Lingam is one of the five Sadakhyas: Shiva Sadakhya, Amurti Sadakhya, Murti Sadakhya, Kartru Sadakhya, and Karma Sadakhya. This Shiva Lingam is the Karma Sadakhya. The Atmartha Puja performed by Shiva devotees with love is of three types: Suddha (pure), Kevala (exclusive), and Mishra (mixed). Worshipping only the Shiva Lingam is Suddha Puja. Worshipping the Shiva Lingam along with Goddess Uma is Kevala Puja. Worshipping Ganesha, Skanda, and Rishabhadeva along with Shiva and Uma, making a total of five, is also Kevala Puja. Worshipping by expanding to include Surya, Ganesha, and ending with Chandeswara, is Mishra Puja. When performing Atmartha Shiva Puja, one should worship with Shodasha Upacharas (sixteen services), or at least Dasha Upacharas (ten services), or at least Pancha Upacharas (five services). Invocation, seat, foot-washing water, offering water, sipping water, bathing, clothing, sacred thread, fragrance, flowers, incense, lamp, food offering, mouth freshener, praise, and circumambulation with prostration are called Shodasha Upacharas, the sixteen services. These sixteen services are also described in another way: seat, invocation, foot-washing water, sipping water, offering water, bathing, clothing, fragrance, flowers, food offering, betel leaf, incense, lamp, Aratrika (waving of lights), sacred ash, and sipping water from the palm. If one cannot perform these, at least ten services should be performed: seat, invocation, foot-washing water, sipping water, offering water, bathing, clothing, fragrance, flowers, and food offering. Or at least five services should be performed: fragrance, flowers, food offering, incense, and lamp. The philosophical meaning of Padya, Achamana, etc., is explained. Padya (foot-washing water) is a symbol of attaining the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. Achamana (sipping water) is a symbol of attaining His gracious divine face. Arghyam (offering water) is a symbol of attaining His sacred head, which is beyond all sounds. Flowers are a symbol of attaining the bliss of liberation. Thirumanjanam (sacred bath) is a symbol of purifying the impurities of the soul. Incense is a symbol of removing the ignorance. The lamp is a symbol of the knowledge that shines when that ignorance is removed. Food offering and mouth freshener are symbols of removing the ego-consciousness, which is the individual self, so that love arises in the mind. This truth of performing these services to Lord Shiva is known through the Sadashiva Roopa Sutras. The Sri Kalottara Agama states that detachment from the world is fragrance, abandoning dualities is flowers. Circumambulation is pervading everywhere through grace along with Shambhu, Lord Shiva. Just as a river merges with the ocean and becomes one with it, when the soul unites with Shiva, the soul attaining the qualities of Shiva is Namaskaram (prostration). Thus, the Sri Kalottara Agama explains the philosophical meaning of these. Now, the Antaryaga Puja (internal worship) is described. The Rig Veda states that when performing external puja, Antaryaga Puja must also be performed. It states that those who grasp the supreme Lord, who resides as the inner controller in people, along with Shiva-Shakti, within themselves, are the ones who consume food with their tongue. The Parasara Purana, which is an Upabrumhana (elaboration) of this mantra, also states: "He who always desires Lord Shiva, who is to be worshipped along with His gracious Shakti, within himself, consumes the nectar-filled ambrosial water with his tongue. He who does not desire Lord Shiva along with His consort Bhavani Shakti within himself, undoubtedly consumes feces with his tongue." When performing Antaryaga Puja, the eight-finger-sized lotus originating from the navel should be considered as the Nivrutti Kala (stage of cessation) in the form of Prithvi (earth). The eight petals of that lotus should be considered as the Pratishtha Kala (stage of establishment) in the form of the 23 Tattvas like Appu (water). The stamens should be considered as the Vidya Kala (stage of knowledge) in the form of Vidya Tattva. The pericarp should be considered as the Shanti Kala (stage of peace) in the form of Suddha Vidya, Ishwara, and Sadakhya. The seeds should be considered as the Shantyateeta Kala (stage beyond peace) in the form of Bindu and Nada. When the 36 Tattvas stand as the seat, for those who are clearly enlightened, as it is said that the Jiva (individual soul) is the Shiva Lingam, worshipping the soul, which has Lord Shiva as its life, as the Lingam, is Antaryaga Puja. Our Thirunavukkarasu Peruman has sung this: "Making the body a temple, the firm mind a servant, truth as purity, the mind a gem-like Lingam, with love as ghee and milk, bathing it with abundant water, we have shown this worship to Ishana." Here, "mind" is taken as a name for the soul, and the soul-Lingam is called the mind-gem-Lingam. Thus, the Antaryaga Puja, described in the Rig Veda mantra, its Upabrumhana, and the Shiva Agamas, is extensively explained in Sivagnana Siddhiyar in the Thiruvirutham that begins with "Antaryagam Thannai Mukti Sadhanama." Thus, as described above, those who do not worship the Shiva Lingam, which is expounded in the Vedas, Shiva Agamas, Smritis, Itihasas, and Puranas, both internally and externally, are subject to birth and death, as stated by Thirunavukkarasu Peruman. He states: "If they do not utter the five-lettered sacred name, if they do not speak of the fiery-hued Lord even once, if they never circumambulate the temple, if they do not pluck flowers and offer them before eating, if they do not smear sacred ash to cure their ailments, what is the purpose of their birth? They are engaged in the work of being born again and again, suffering from great diseases and dying." Therefore, it is explained that Shiva Lingam puja is indispensable for those who desire liberation. The greatness of the Shiva Lingam, described in the Vedas, Shiva Agamas, Smritis, Itihasas, Puranas, Thirumurais, and Shastras, has been compiled and presented to us in the book "Shiva Linga Mahatmyam" by Mangaiyarkarasi Perumatti. Divine Sekkizhar Peruman has embedded all these profound meanings in his verse, "Kaanatha Aruvinukkum Uruvinukkum Karanamai Neelnagam Anindharkku Nikarkuriyam Shivalingam," as pointed out by great scholars. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 08 — Sakkiya Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 922, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Sakkiya Nayanar Puranam. The 12th and 13th verses. He continued the duty of the service he had begun, without ceasing, and seeing that, he threw stones. He did not abandon the Buddhist attire. He understood that all actions belonged to Pashupati. Therefore, the great ascetic, with devotion, performed this discipline without fail. If one were to describe the nature of this service performed by the Nayanar, it is a great puja for the pure Lord, born of true love and performed according to the custom of the path of love. Thus, from the first day he began to worship the Shiva Lingam by throwing stones, he realized that it was the grace of the Lord that caused it to happen. His love overflowed, and he continued this as an uninterrupted, disciplined duty. Seeing that this was an act born of grace, he continued to worship the Shiva Lingam by throwing stones, without fail, according to this discipline. He did not abandon the Buddhist attire, which is a saffron robe. The great ascetic Nayanar clearly understood the truth that all actions are the gracious acts of Pashupati, Lord Shiva. Therefore, he performed this disciplined act with great love. If one were to describe the nature of this service performed by the Nayanar, it is a great puja for the pure Lord, born of true love and performed according to the custom of the path of love. Pashupati, being the Lord of all beings, we see here the nature of the Lord who guides souls according to their maturity. This practice is a practice of the path of love. There are two types of practices: the path of rules and the path of love. Sakkiya Nayanar followed the path of love. Although it seems to transcend rules, since the Lord accepts it out of love, it belongs to the path of love. The 14th verse. If one sees that even the act of throwing stones was out of love, then the sandal-beaten feet of the hunter with the bow were placed on His sacred head. Good people will consider his actions as born of love and desire them, but others will call them stones. For Arana, they are flowers. This is a very beautiful verse. If one examines the act of throwing stones and sees that it was a service performed out of love, then we have seen how the sandal-beaten feet of Thirukannappa Nayanar, the hunter with the bow, were placed on the Lord's sacred head. Here, similarly, others would call the actions of Sakkiya Nayanar, his service, as merely throwing stones. But for Lord Shiva, they become flowers. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman here uses the incident of Thirukannappa Devanayanar, the hunter with the bow, whose sandal-beaten feet touched the Lord's sacred head, as an example to illustrate this loving service performed by Sakkiya Nayanar. We saw in the Thirukannappa Devanayanar Puranam, "He replaced the flower on His head with the golden sandal that bent his foot." Since "seruppadi" (sandal-beaten feet) is mentioned, we should not only consider that, but also include the other actions and services performed by Thirukannappa Devanayanar, such as spitting mouth-water, adorning the Lord with a garland of flowers tucked in his hair, and offering meat as food. All these were accepted and approved by the Lord with great joy as appropriate. This service performed by Thirukannappa Devanayanar was praised by great saints like Manikkavasagar Peruman of Thiruvathavur. Therefore, those who do not follow the path of love will call the stones thrown by Sakkiya Nayanar as mere stones. But for the Lord, they become flowers. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman praises this loving act of Sakkiya Nayanar by saying, "He made the stones thrown by the Buddha, who never forgot, into fresh flowers." If one calls what he threw as stones, it becomes a slander against Lord Shiva. Slandering a devotee is slandering Arana. It is also slandering the compassion of Arana, which is the sweet nectar born of love. Thus ends this verse. The 15th verse. One day, by His grace, he forgot and was about to eat. "I forgot my Lord without knowing," he said, and rising with overflowing love, he quickly set out and appeared before the Lord, who wears the hide of a white elephant. One day, while following this disciplined practice, Sakkiya Nayanar, by divine grace, forgot to throw stones at the Lord and was about to eat. Such forgetfulness of a discipline is also bestowed by the Lord. As he was about to eat, he remembered. Our Sakkiya Nayanar was one who showed unwavering love. If he made a mistake now, it must surely be due to divine grace. Similarly, when he remembered again, that too happened by divine grace. Forgetting and then remembering immediately is the stage when one is about to attain the Lord's feet, meaning Sakkiya Nayanar is about to attain Sayujya Mukti (union with the Lord). That day was therefore such that divine grace caused both forgetting and remembering. Having forgotten earlier, he remembered and realized that he had erred and broken his discipline. Agitated by this, he quickly set out with great eagerness and swiftly arrived before the Shiva Lingam, where the Lord resides. The 16th and 17th verses. He picked up a stone and threw it with precision. The Lord with the frontal eye, who had seen his desire to escape the deed of eating and his fear, appeared before the devotee in the vast sky with His consort, showering gracious glances. He came riding on a bull, and the devotee, who had attained His feet, saw Him, prostrated with folded hands, and the Lord, bestowing a gracious glance, granted him eternal service in the supreme Shiva Loka and departed. These are the verses. The Nayanar, who appeared before the Lord's divine body, picked up a stone that was there, and leaving aside even the act of eating, he came running with fear and great desire to see Him. The Lord with the frontal eye, seeing him, showered gracious glances and appeared before the devotee in the vast sky with His consort, Goddess Uma. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the stone picked up by the Nayanar as "oru kal" (a stone). "Oru kal" can also mean "a unique stone." If interpreted that way, the stone the Nayanar picked up that day was a unique stone. At that moment, Lord Shiva appeared, and the Nayanar saw Him. Therefore, this act of throwing stones concludes here, and the Nayanar is about to join Shiva Loka. It was a unique stone that supported this, hence "oru kal." It was a stone that had a special significance compared to the stones the Nayanar had thrown on previous days. The Lord, who saw the Nayanar's act of throwing the stone with the intention of understanding Shiva as the means to salvation by His grace, and the desire within it, now decided that the time had come to bestow grace, having observed the Nayanar's actions until now. He appeared in the vast sky, not the physical sky but the sky of knowledge, with Goddess Uma, who is the power of grace, showering gracious glances. The speed with which the Nayanar ran signifies his maturity to escape from the cruel cycle of birth and the consequences of his actions and to seek refuge at the Lord's feet. Seeing this, the Lord appeared there. The unique act of the Lord, who appeared on a young bull with Goddess Uma. The Nayanar, who had attained the Lord's feet, saw Him, prostrated with folded hands, and the Lord, bestowing a gracious glance, granted him eternal service in the supreme Shiva Loka, which is superior to all other abodes, and departed. The Lord united the Nayanar with Shiva Loka, which is superior to all other abodes. As per the saying, "I desired to see the assembly of ancient devotees," and as stated in Kandar Kalivenba, "He sent me and united me with the ancient devotees, and uplifted me to the supreme abode," the Nayanar joined Shiva Loka and is enjoying bliss. Thus, having narrated the sacred history of Sakkiya Nayanar attaining the Lord's feet, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman concludes with the 18th verse. He sings, "Only the Effulgent One knows the intention of the flawless, excellent devotee who daily threw stones and approached the Primeval One. What else can one say? I have placed His feet on my head and, with their help, I am about to narrate the history of Sirappuli Nayanar, thereby removing evil." Thus, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman concludes the sacred history of Sakkiya Nayanar and begins to narrate the sacred history of Sirappuli Nayanar. We too will continue to contemplate. Hail, hail to the sacred feet of Sakkiya Nayanar. Hail, hail to the sacred feet of Sundaramurti Swamigal. Hail, hail to the sacred feet of Nambiyandar Nambigal. Hail, hail to the sacred feet of the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Hail, hail to the sacred feet of the commentator. Thiruchitrambalam.
